I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.

It’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.

It started around when the new regional manager came in and started really riding me… taking accounts I’d had for years. I mean, those were people I knew, not just names on a spreadsheet. Anyway, yeah—about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to look forward to bowling night, messing around in the garden, taking my wife to those little gallery shows she likes… we even used to go dancing sometimes, back when my knees weren’t the whole conversation. \n\nNow when something comes up, I just sort of sit there and think of reasons not to go. I tell her I’m tired, or maybe next time, or the yard can wait… but honestly, none of it feels worth the effort anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big speech about it, but I’ll wake up sometimes and think… maybe my wife and the kids would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And then I feel ashamed for even thinking that, because my wife—she’s put up with me through a lot, you know, even back when I’d come home talking too much about work and she’d just nod along like she understood all the customer drama.\n\nBut yeah… those thoughts have been there. And they scare me, honestly. I don’t trust myself the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: No… not really back to normal. It’s been pretty much this whole stretch, about eight months now, since that new regional manager came in and started coming down on me. \n\nI mean, at first I kept thinking it was just a rough week, you know? Sales has rough weeks. I’ve had managers before who thought yelling was leadership. But then he started taking accounts I’d handled for years, people I’d built up relationships with… and it just never really lifted after that. Maybe I’ve had a decent afternoon here or there, but not like I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how brief, where you felt completely free of these depressed feelings during the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Completely free of it? No… I don’t think so. It’s been hanging around the whole time, about eight months now.\n\nIt really started after that new regional manager came in and started riding me all the time—taking away accounts I’d had for years, people I knew by name, knew their kids’ names sometimes. I know that sounds like work stuff, but… it just all seemed to start there and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this way in your life, before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? I’ve had rough times before, sure. Sales’ll do that to you—one month you’re everyone’s favorite guy, next month you can’t get someone to return a call, and you start taking it home with you.\n\nBut feeling like this? For this long? I’m not sure. I can’t really remember another time where it was… the same as this. Maybe I’m not answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big speech about it, but I’ll wake up sometimes and think… maybe my wife and the kids would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And then I feel ashamed for even thinking that, because my wife—she’s put up with me through a lot, you know, even back when I’d come home talking too much about work and she’d just nod along like she understood all the customer drama.\n\nBut yeah… those thoughts have been there. And they scare me, honestly. I don’t trust myself the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: No… not really back to normal. It’s been pretty much this whole stretch, about eight months now, since that new regional manager came in and started coming down on me. \n\nI mean, at first I kept thinking it was just a rough week, you know? Sales has rough weeks. I’ve had managers before who thought yelling was leadership. But then he started taking accounts I’d handled for years, people I’d built up relationships with… and it just never really lifted after that. Maybe I’ve had a decent afternoon here or there, but not like I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how brief, where you felt completely free of these depressed feelings during the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Completely free of it? No… I don’t think so. It’s been hanging around the whole time, about eight months now.\n\nIt really started after that new regional manager came in and started riding me all the time—taking away accounts I’d had for years, people I knew by name, knew their kids’ names sometimes. I know that sounds like work stuff, but… it just all seemed to start there and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this way in your life, before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? I’ve had rough times before, sure. Sales’ll do that to you—one month you’re everyone’s favorite guy, next month you can’t get someone to return a call, and you start taking it home with you.\n\nBut feeling like this? For this long? I’m not sure. I can’t really remember another time where it was… the same as this. Maybe I’m not answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… I don’t know, I don’t think so. I mean, life has had its rough patches, sure—when work was slow, or when money got tight, you kind of carry it around for a while. In sales, you can have a bad month and start thinking the whole world’s against you.\n\nBut an actual episode like this, lasting weeks? I can’t really point to one. Not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist diagnosed as depression?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never had a doctor or therapist sit me down and say, “This is depression,” before this.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been the kind of guy who runs to appointments for every little thing—my wife used to get on me about that, saying I’d rather complain about something for six months than make a phone call. And with work, you just kind of keep going, you know? Sales doesn’t really care if you’re having a rough time.\n\nBut no, as far as an actual diagnosis from somebody, I don’t have anything like that before now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I know that sounds like work stuff, but… it just all seemed to start there and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this way in your life, before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? I’ve had rough times before, sure. Sales’ll do that to you—one month you’re everyone’s favorite guy, next month you can’t get someone to return a call, and you start taking it home with you.\n\nBut feeling like this? For this long? I’m not sure. I can’t really remember another time where it was… the same as this. Maybe I’m not answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… I don’t know, I don’t think so. I mean, life has had its rough patches, sure—when work was slow, or when money got tight, you kind of carry it around for a while. In sales, you can have a bad month and start thinking the whole world’s against you.\n\nBut an actual episode like this, lasting weeks? I can’t really point to one. Not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist diagnosed as depression?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never had a doctor or therapist sit me down and say, “This is depression,” before this.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been the kind of guy who runs to appointments for every little thing—my wife used to get on me about that, saying I’d rather complain about something for six months than make a phone call. And with work, you just kind of keep going, you know? Sales doesn’t really care if you’re having a rough time.\n\nBut no, as far as an actual diagnosis from somebody, I don’t have anything like that before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, right? You have a bad quarter, boss is breathing down your neck, car needs work, suddenly the dishwasher goes too—those kinds of things pile up.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t think so. This feels different enough that I would’ve remembered it, I think. My wife probably would’ve remembered too, she was always better at keeping track of what was going on with me than I was. But no, I can’t really think of an earlier time that felt like this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… I don’t know, I don’t think so. I mean, life has had its rough patches, sure—when work was slow, or when money got tight, you kind of carry it around for a while. In sales, you can have a bad month and start thinking the whole world’s against you.\n\nBut an actual episode like this, lasting weeks? I can’t really point to one. Not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist diagnosed as depression?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never had a doctor or therapist sit me down and say, “This is depression,” before this.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been the kind of guy who runs to appointments for every little thing—my wife used to get on me about that, saying I’d rather complain about something for six months than make a phone call. And with work, you just kind of keep going, you know? Sales doesn’t really care if you’re having a rough time.\n\nBut no, as far as an actual diagnosis from somebody, I don’t have anything like that before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, right? You have a bad quarter, boss is breathing down your neck, car needs work, suddenly the dishwasher goes too—those kinds of things pile up.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t think so. This feels different enough that I would’ve remembered it, I think. My wife probably would’ve remembered too, she was always better at keeping track of what was going on with me than I was. But no, I can’t really think of an earlier time that felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a mental health professional treated?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I never really had someone in that field treat me for this before.\n\nI’ve talked to people about stress here and there—well, not professionals, more like coworkers over coffee or my wife when she’d catch me staring at bills at the kitchen table. She always said I’d talk in circles before I’d just say what was bothering me. But as far as a counselor or psychiatrist actually treating me for depression before this… no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure. I can’t really remember another time where it was… the same as this. Maybe I’m not answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… I don’t know, I don’t think so. I mean, life has had its rough patches, sure—when work was slow, or when money got tight, you kind of carry it around for a while. In sales, you can have a bad month and start thinking the whole world’s against you.\n\nBut an actual episode like this, lasting weeks? I can’t really point to one. Not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist diagnosed as depression?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never had a doctor or therapist sit me down and say, “This is depression,” before this.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been the kind of guy who runs to appointments for every little thing—my wife used to get on me about that, saying I’d rather complain about something for six months than make a phone call. And with work, you just kind of keep going, you know? Sales doesn’t really care if you’re having a rough time.\n\nBut no, as far as an actual diagnosis from somebody, I don’t have anything like that before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, right? You have a bad quarter, boss is breathing down your neck, car needs work, suddenly the dishwasher goes too—those kinds of things pile up.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t think so. This feels different enough that I would’ve remembered it, I think. My wife probably would’ve remembered too, she was always better at keeping track of what was going on with me than I was. But no, I can’t really think of an earlier time that felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a mental health professional treated?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I never really had someone in that field treat me for this before.\n\nI’ve talked to people about stress here and there—well, not professionals, more like coworkers over coffee or my wife when she’d catch me staring at bills at the kitchen table. She always said I’d talk in circles before I’d just say what was bothering me. But as far as a counselor or psychiatrist actually treating me for depression before this… no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not by a mental health professional, no. I never really had that kind of treatment before.\n\nI guess looking back, there were times I probably should’ve talked to somebody—my wife used to say I’d rather reorganize the garage than admit something was bothering me. And then I’d get caught up in work, driving all over for clients, making calls, all that noise… you just keep moving.\n\nBut no, not another episode that was treated by a counselor or psychiatrist or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s been pretty much this whole stretch, about eight months now, since that new regional manager came in and started coming down on me. \n\nI mean, at first I kept thinking it was just a rough week, you know? Sales has rough weeks. I’ve had managers before who thought yelling was leadership. But then he started taking accounts I’d handled for years, people I’d built up relationships with… and it just never really lifted after that. Maybe I’ve had a decent afternoon here or there, but not like I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how brief, where you felt completely free of these depressed feelings during the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Completely free of it? No… I don’t think so. It’s been hanging around the whole time, about eight months now.\n\nIt really started after that new regional manager came in and started riding me all the time—taking away accounts I’d had for years, people I knew by name, knew their kids’ names sometimes. I know that sounds like work stuff, but… it just all seemed to start there and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this way in your life, before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? I’ve had rough times before, sure. Sales’ll do that to you—one month you’re everyone’s favorite guy, next month you can’t get someone to return a call, and you start taking it home with you.\n\nBut feeling like this? For this long? I’m not sure. I can’t really remember another time where it was… the same as this. Maybe I’m not answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… I don’t know, I don’t think so. I mean, life has had its rough patches, sure—when work was slow, or when money got tight, you kind of carry it around for a while. In sales, you can have a bad month and start thinking the whole world’s against you.\n\nBut an actual episode like this, lasting weeks? I can’t really point to one. Not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist diagnosed as depression?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never had a doctor or therapist sit me down and say, “This is depression,” before this.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been the kind of guy who runs to appointments for every little thing—my wife used to get on me about that, saying I’d rather complain about something for six months than make a phone call. And with work, you just kind of keep going, you know? Sales doesn’t really care if you’re having a rough time.\n\nBut no, as far as an actual diagnosis from somebody, I don’t have anything like that before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, right? You have a bad quarter, boss is breathing down your neck, car needs work, suddenly the dishwasher goes too—those kinds of things pile up.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t think so. This feels different enough that I would’ve remembered it, I think. My wife probably would’ve remembered too, she was always better at keeping track of what was going on with me than I was. But no, I can’t really think of an earlier time that felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a mental health professional treated?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I never really had someone in that field treat me for this before.\n\nI’ve talked to people about stress here and there—well, not professionals, more like coworkers over coffee or my wife when she’d catch me staring at bills at the kitchen table. She always said I’d talk in circles before I’d just say what was bothering me. But as far as a counselor or psychiatrist actually treating me for depression before this… no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not by a mental health professional, no. I never really had that kind of treatment before.\n\nI guess looking back, there were times I probably should’ve talked to somebody—my wife used to say I’d rather reorganize the garage than admit something was bothering me. And then I’d get caught up in work, driving all over for clients, making calls, all that noise… you just keep moving.\n\nBut no, not another episode that was treated by a counselor or psychiatrist or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now, but then got better on their own or with treatment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this.\n\nI’ve had rough patches that eased up, sure—usually once whatever mess was going on passed. Like a bad stretch at work, money getting tight, family things… you know how one problem turns into five before lunch. But those didn’t feel the same as this does now.\n\nSo if you mean something that felt like this and then just lifted on its own, or because somebody treated it… no, I can’t really point to anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It’s hard to explain. I used to be Ruben, you know, the guy who could talk to anybody—customers, neighbors, whoever—and actually enjoy the day. That was kind of my thing in sales, being able to chat with people.\n\nLately it feels like that old version of me is just… gone. Like I’m there, but not really there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It started around when the new regional manager came in and started really riding me… taking accounts I’d had for years. I mean, those were people I knew, not just names on a spreadsheet. Anyway, yeah—about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to look forward to bowling night, messing around in the garden, taking my wife to those little gallery shows she likes… we even used to go dancing sometimes, back when my knees weren’t the whole conversation. \n\nNow when something comes up, I just sort of sit there and think of reasons not to go. I tell her I’m tired, or maybe next time, or the yard can wait… but honestly, none of it feels worth the effort anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big speech about it, but I’ll wake up sometimes and think… maybe my wife and the kids would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And then I feel ashamed for even thinking that, because my wife—she’s put up with me through a lot, you know, even back when I’d come home talking too much about work and she’d just nod along like she understood all the customer drama.\n\nBut yeah… those thoughts have been there. And they scare me, honestly. I don’t trust myself the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: No… not really back to normal. It’s been pretty much this whole stretch, about eight months now, since that new regional manager came in and started coming down on me. \n\nI mean, at first I kept thinking it was just a rough week, you know? Sales has rough weeks. I’ve had managers before who thought yelling was leadership. But then he started taking accounts I’d handled for years, people I’d built up relationships with… and it just never really lifted after that. Maybe I’ve had a decent afternoon here or there, but not like I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how brief, where you felt completely free of these depressed feelings during the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Completely free of it? No… I don’t think so. It’s been hanging around the whole time, about eight months now.\n\nIt really started after that new regional manager came in and started riding me all the time—taking away accounts I’d had for years, people I knew by name, knew their kids’ names sometimes. I know that sounds like work stuff, but… it just all seemed to start there and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this way in your life, before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? I’ve had rough times before, sure. Sales’ll do that to you—one month you’re everyone’s favorite guy, next month you can’t get someone to return a call, and you start taking it home with you.\n\nBut feeling like this? For this long? I’m not sure. I can’t really remember another time where it was… the same as this. Maybe I’m not answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… I don’t know, I don’t think so. I mean, life has had its rough patches, sure—when work was slow, or when money got tight, you kind of carry it around for a while. In sales, you can have a bad month and start thinking the whole world’s against you.\n\nBut an actual episode like this, lasting weeks? I can’t really point to one. Not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist diagnosed as depression?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never had a doctor or therapist sit me down and say, “This is depression,” before this.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been the kind of guy who runs to appointments for every little thing—my wife used to get on me about that, saying I’d rather complain about something for six months than make a phone call. And with work, you just kind of keep going, you know? Sales doesn’t really care if you’re having a rough time.\n\nBut no, as far as an actual diagnosis from somebody, I don’t have anything like that before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, right? You have a bad quarter, boss is breathing down your neck, car needs work, suddenly the dishwasher goes too—those kinds of things pile up.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t think so. This feels different enough that I would’ve remembered it, I think. My wife probably would’ve remembered too, she was always better at keeping track of what was going on with me than I was. But no, I can’t really think of an earlier time that felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a mental health professional treated?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I never really had someone in that field treat me for this before.\n\nI’ve talked to people about stress here and there—well, not professionals, more like coworkers over coffee or my wife when she’d catch me staring at bills at the kitchen table. She always said I’d talk in circles before I’d just say what was bothering me. But as far as a counselor or psychiatrist actually treating me for depression before this… no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not by a mental health professional, no. I never really had that kind of treatment before.\n\nI guess looking back, there were times I probably should’ve talked to somebody—my wife used to say I’d rather reorganize the garage than admit something was bothering me. And then I’d get caught up in work, driving all over for clients, making calls, all that noise… you just keep moving.\n\nBut no, not another episode that was treated by a counselor or psychiatrist or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now, but then got better on their own or with treatment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this.\n\nI’ve had rough patches that eased up, sure—usually once whatever mess was going on passed. Like a bad stretch at work, money getting tight, family things… you know how one problem turns into five before lunch. But those didn’t feel the same as this does now.\n\nSo if you mean something that felt like this and then just lifted on its own, or because somebody treated it… no, I can’t really point to anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have an episode of depression before this one that was treated and went away, but then came back?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I had one like that—treated, went away, then came back. I mean, I’ve had ups and downs like anybody, and I’d tell myself it was just work or getting older or whatever. In sales you kind of learn to smile through a lot, even when you’re sitting in the car outside a client’s office wondering how you’re supposed to keep everything straight.\n\nBut no… if you mean something I actually got treated for and then it returned, I don’t have that history.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t think so… I mean, not that I can clearly pick out.

I know that sounds like work stuff, but… it just all seemed to start there and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this way in your life, before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? I’ve had rough times before, sure. Sales’ll do that to you—one month you’re everyone’s favorite guy, next month you can’t get someone to return a call, and you start taking it home with you.\n\nBut feeling like this? For this long? I’m not sure. I can’t really remember another time where it was… the same as this. Maybe I’m not answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… I don’t know, I don’t think so. I mean, life has had its rough patches, sure—when work was slow, or when money got tight, you kind of carry it around for a while. In sales, you can have a bad month and start thinking the whole world’s against you.\n\nBut an actual episode like this, lasting weeks? I can’t really point to one. Not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist diagnosed as depression?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never had a doctor or therapist sit me down and say, “This is depression,” before this.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been the kind of guy who runs to appointments for every little thing—my wife used to get on me about that, saying I’d rather complain about something for six months than make a phone call. And with work, you just kind of keep going, you know? Sales doesn’t really care if you’re having a rough time.\n\nBut no, as far as an actual diagnosis from somebody, I don’t have anything like that before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, right? You have a bad quarter, boss is breathing down your neck, car needs work, suddenly the dishwasher goes too—those kinds of things pile up.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t think so. This feels different enough that I would’ve remembered it, I think. My wife probably would’ve remembered too, she was always better at keeping track of what was going on with me than I was. But no, I can’t really think of an earlier time that felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a mental health professional treated?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I never really had someone in that field treat me for this before.\n\nI’ve talked to people about stress here and there—well, not professionals, more like coworkers over coffee or my wife when she’d catch me staring at bills at the kitchen table. She always said I’d talk in circles before I’d just say what was bothering me. But as far as a counselor or psychiatrist actually treating me for depression before this… no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not by a mental health professional, no. I never really had that kind of treatment before.\n\nI guess looking back, there were times I probably should’ve talked to somebody—my wife used to say I’d rather reorganize the garage than admit something was bothering me. And then I’d get caught up in work, driving all over for clients, making calls, all that noise… you just keep moving.\n\nBut no, not another episode that was treated by a counselor or psychiatrist or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now, but then got better on their own or with treatment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this.\n\nI’ve had rough patches that eased up, sure—usually once whatever mess was going on passed. Like a bad stretch at work, money getting tight, family things… you know how one problem turns into five before lunch. But those didn’t feel the same as this does now.\n\nSo if you mean something that felt like this and then just lifted on its own, or because somebody treated it… no, I can’t really point to anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have an episode of depression before this one that was treated and went away, but then came back?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I had one like that—treated, went away, then came back. I mean, I’ve had ups and downs like anybody, and I’d tell myself it was just work or getting older or whatever. In sales you kind of learn to smile through a lot, even when you’re sitting in the car outside a client’s office wondering how you’re supposed to keep everything straight.\n\nBut no… if you mean something I actually got treated for and then it returned, I don’t have that history.\n\nDoctor: Did you consider the previous episodes of feeling down as the same kind of depression you are experiencing now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would’ve called them the same thing, no… I mean, at the time I just thought I was worn out or frustrated.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to have days where I’d come home from driving around all day and just sit in the driveway for a minute before going inside, but then there was always dinner, bills, some customer calling about something that couldn’t possibly wait until morning. So I didn’t really stop and compare it, you know?\n\nThis now feels… I don’t know. I don’t think those earlier times were the same, but I may not be the best judge of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any earlier episodes of depression that were similar to this one and then went away before this episode started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I can clearly pick out.\n\nThere were times before where I felt low, sure, but it was usually tied up with other things—work being slow, customers changing their minds after I’d spent half the week chasing them, stuff at home piling up. And then I’d get busy again or something would shift, and I guess I’d just move on.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I had something like this that went away and then this started. At least not that I recognized that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

And then I’d get busy again or something would shift, and I guess I’d just move on.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I had something like this that went away and then this started. At least not that I recognized that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist said would likely happen again?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / sales_representative_of_services / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think anyone ever told me that. I mean, I haven’t really had that kind of conversation before, where a doctor or therapist said, “This is probably going to come back.”

Most of the time, honestly, I just kept going. You know, in sales you get used to saying you’re fine because nobody wants to hear your whole life story when you’re supposed to be talking contracts or service plans. But no… not that I can remember. No one warned me about anything like that.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It’s hard to explain. I used to be Ruben, you know, the guy who could talk to anybody—customers, neighbors, whoever—and actually enjoy the day. That was kind of my thing in sales, being able to chat with people.\n\nLately it feels like that old version of me is just… gone. Like I’m there, but not really there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It started around when the new regional manager came in and started really riding me… taking accounts I’d had for years. I mean, those were people I knew, not just names on a spreadsheet. Anyway, yeah—about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to look forward to bowling night, messing around in the garden, taking my wife to those little gallery shows she likes… we even used to go dancing sometimes, back when my knees weren’t the whole conversation. \n\nNow when something comes up, I just sort of sit there and think of reasons not to go. I tell her I’m tired, or maybe next time, or the yard can wait… but honestly, none of it feels worth the effort anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big speech about it, but I’ll wake up sometimes and think… maybe my wife and the kids would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And then I feel ashamed for even thinking that, because my wife—she’s put up with me through a lot, you know, even back when I’d come home talking too much about work and she’d just nod along like she understood all the customer drama.\n\nBut yeah… those thoughts have been there. And they scare me, honestly. I don’t trust myself the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: No… not really back to normal. It’s been pretty much this whole stretch, about eight months now, since that new regional manager came in and started coming down on me. \n\nI mean, at first I kept thinking it was just a rough week, you know? Sales has rough weeks. I’ve had managers before who thought yelling was leadership. But then he started taking accounts I’d handled for years, people I’d built up relationships with… and it just never really lifted after that. Maybe I’ve had a decent afternoon here or there, but not like I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how brief, where you felt completely free of these depressed feelings during the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Completely free of it? No… I don’t think so. It’s been hanging around the whole time, about eight months now.\n\nIt really started after that new regional manager came in and started riding me all the time—taking away accounts I’d had for years, people I knew by name, knew their kids’ names sometimes. I know that sounds like work stuff, but… it just all seemed to start there and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this way in your life, before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? I’ve had rough times before, sure. Sales’ll do that to you—one month you’re everyone’s favorite guy, next month you can’t get someone to return a call, and you start taking it home with you.\n\nBut feeling like this? For this long? I’m not sure. I can’t really remember another time where it was… the same as this. Maybe I’m not answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. But this kind of low… waking up most days with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody, joke with clients, enjoy a day—like he’s just gone… I don’t remember feeling that before these last months.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the guy people would send into a room when things were tense, you know? I could smooth things over. Now I wake up and it’s like there’s nothing there to reach for. So no, not like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been more than one like this. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everybody does, especially in sales, you know, one bad call can ruin your whole afternoon—but this is different.\n\nThis time I came in because most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the old Ruben… the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day… like he’s just gone. I don’t remember another time in my life where it felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant depression before this 8-month period?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant like this. I mean, I’ve had stretches where work was terrible or I felt kind of beat up by life, and I’d go home and complain, maybe snap at people, that kind of thing.\n\nBut before these eight months? I can’t point to a time and say, yeah, that was the same thing. My memory’s not perfect, you know—after 58 years everything kind of blends together, jobs, bills, kids growing up, all of it—but no, I don’t think I had a period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… I don’t know, I don’t think so. I mean, life has had its rough patches, sure—when work was slow, or when money got tight, you kind of carry it around for a while. In sales, you can have a bad month and start thinking the whole world’s against you.\n\nBut an actual episode like this, lasting weeks? I can’t really point to one. Not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist diagnosed as depression?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never had a doctor or therapist sit me down and say, “This is depression,” before this.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been the kind of guy who runs to appointments for every little thing—my wife used to get on me about that, saying I’d rather complain about something for six months than make a phone call. And with work, you just kind of keep going, you know? Sales doesn’t really care if you’re having a rough time.\n\nBut no, as far as an actual diagnosis from somebody, I don’t have anything like that before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I’ve been down before, sure. Everybody has, right? You have a bad quarter, boss is breathing down your neck, car needs work, suddenly the dishwasher goes too—those kinds of things pile up.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t think so. This feels different enough that I would’ve remembered it, I think. My wife probably would’ve remembered too, she was always better at keeping track of what was going on with me than I was. But no, I can’t really think of an earlier time that felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that a mental health professional treated?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I never really had someone in that field treat me for this before.\n\nI’ve talked to people about stress here and there—well, not professionals, more like coworkers over coffee or my wife when she’d catch me staring at bills at the kitchen table. She always said I’d talk in circles before I’d just say what was bothering me. But as far as a counselor or psychiatrist actually treating me for depression before this… no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not by a mental health professional, no. I never really had that kind of treatment before.\n\nI guess looking back, there were times I probably should’ve talked to somebody—my wife used to say I’d rather reorganize the garage than admit something was bothering me. And then I’d get caught up in work, driving all over for clients, making calls, all that noise… you just keep moving.\n\nBut no, not another episode that was treated by a counselor or psychiatrist or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this one that felt similar to what you're experiencing now, but then got better on their own or with treatment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like this.\n\nI’ve had rough patches that eased up, sure—usually once whatever mess was going on passed. Like a bad stretch at work, money getting tight, family things… you know how one problem turns into five before lunch. But those didn’t feel the same as this does now.\n\nSo if you mean something that felt like this and then just lifted on its own, or because somebody treated it… no, I can’t really point to anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have an episode of depression before this one that was treated and went away, but then came back?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I had one like that—treated, went away, then came back. I mean, I’ve had ups and downs like anybody, and I’d tell myself it was just work or getting older or whatever. In sales you kind of learn to smile through a lot, even when you’re sitting in the car outside a client’s office wondering how you’re supposed to keep everything straight.\n\nBut no… if you mean something I actually got treated for and then it returned, I don’t have that history.\n\nDoctor: Did you consider the previous episodes of feeling down as the same kind of depression you are experiencing now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would’ve called them the same thing, no… I mean, at the time I just thought I was worn out or frustrated.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to have days where I’d come home from driving around all day and just sit in the driveway for a minute before going inside, but then there was always dinner, bills, some customer calling about something that couldn’t possibly wait until morning. So I didn’t really stop and compare it, you know?\n\nThis now feels… I don’t know. I don’t think those earlier times were the same, but I may not be the best judge of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any earlier episodes of depression that were similar to this one and then went away before this episode started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I can clearly pick out.\n\nThere were times before where I felt low, sure, but it was usually tied up with other things—work being slow, customers changing their minds after I’d spent half the week chasing them, stuff at home piling up. And then I’d get busy again or something would shift, and I guess I’d just move on.\n\nBut similar to this? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I had something like this that went away and then this started. At least not that I recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any episodes of depression before this one that a doctor or therapist said would likely happen again?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone ever told me that. I mean, I haven’t really had that kind of conversation before, where a doctor or therapist said, “This is probably going to come back.”\n\nMost of the time, honestly, I just kept going. You know, in sales you get used to saying you’re fine because nobody wants to hear your whole life story when you’re supposed to be talking contracts or service plans. But no… not that I can remember. No one warned me about anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

          "suicidal thoughts"

        "count": 3
      }
    ],
    "duration_verified": "symptoms present for over 8 months",
    "functional_impairment": true,
    "traumatic_stressor": null,
    "psychosocial_stressor": true,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "Onset of symptoms after a significant psychosocial stressor (new regional manager at work)",
      "No prior episodes of similar depression"
    ]
  }
}
